--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---

[essai 1/2]
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
The goal is to get robots into the BLUE-walled strip (black floor). The score increases for every robot outside that region. So robots need to find and stay in the black-floored strip near blue walls.

**Key observations:**
1. The best performer is pure Repulsion (B=5) - this is surprising but makes sense if robots repel each other and spread out, some end up in the target zone.
2. GoToColor targeting color 1 (red? or blue?) also works well (score 16087).
3. The target region has BLACK floor and BLUE walls.
4. Robots can detect floor color (black=0, gray=1, white=2) and wall colors via camera.

**Strategy:**
- Robots should move toward blue walls (GoToColor with blue color)
- Once on black floor, they should stay (stop or slow exploration)
- The champion uses repulsion which spreads robots out

**Color mapping:** Looking at the conditions, C=7 perceives a color with camera. Colors: {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Blue walls border the target. Color 6 might be cyan, 5 magenta, 4 yellow... Actually the standard colors in ARGoS are typically: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

So GoToColor with clr=3 (blue) should move toward blue walls!

**Plan:**
1. **Survive**: Copy champion (repulsion, score 15742.88)
2. **Exploit champion**: Vary repulsion gain slightly
3. **Exploit #2**: GoToColor blue walls (clr=3) 
4. **Exploit #3**: 2-state machines combining floor detection with GoToColor
5. **Explore**: Multi-state machines with black floor detection → stop behavior

FSM designs:
- 2-state: GoToColor(blue) → when on black floor → stop/exploration
- 2-state: Exploration → when black floor → stop
- GoToColor blue directly

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 5 --rep0 3.6193 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 5 --p0x0 0.0

Wait, I need at least 1 transition per state... Actually, looking at the champion format: `--nstates 1 --s0 5 --rep0 3.6193 --cle0 0` - no transitions listed! Let me re-check. The champion has no `--n0` parameter. Let me assume a state with 0 transitions is valid (it loops forever).

Actually looking at format: `--n<I> T : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4]`. But the champion doesn't have this... Maybe 0 is allowed or it's omitted when 0. I'll follow the champion format for the copy.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 5 --rep0 3.6193 --cle0 0

Now let me design the other 13:

[essai 2/2]
The champion uses pure repulsion which spreads robots but doesn't direct them to the blue-walled black-floor strip. I'll try GoToColor targeting blue (clr=3), floor-reactive 2-state machines that stop on black floor, and variants of the repulsion champion.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 3717) is pure GoToColor targeting blue (clr=3), which makes robots move toward blue walls and congregate in the blue-walled black-floor strip. This is dramatically better than repulsion. Now I need to exploit this and add stop-on-black-floor behavior to keep robots in the target zone.

Key insight: GoToColor blue is excellent. Adding a transition to stop when on black floor (condition C=0) could lock robots in place once they arrive. Also, the 2-state explore+GoToBlue machine scored 5177, suggesting exploration helps find blue walls when not visible.

**Plan:**
- Survive: copy champion
- Exploit: 2-state GoToBlue → Stop-on-black, with varied probabilities
- Exploit: add a transition back from stop when gray floor detected
- Explore: 3-state machines with exploration, GoToBlue, and stop phases
- Try GoAwayColor green (clr=2) as alternative direction cue

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is still pure GoToColor blue (3716). The second best (3803) uses GoAwayColor green + GoToColor blue with floor detection, which is nearly as good. The pattern emerging: robots need to move toward blue walls, and stopping on black floor hurts (scores 9253, 9796). The best multi-state machines use GoToBlue → stop-on-black → explore-back, but the stop state causes problems if robots leave. The key insight is that GoAwayColor green (clr=2) combined with GoToColor blue works well since green walls are on the opposite side.

I'll focus exploitation on: (1) pure GoToBlue variants, (2) GoAwayGreen+GoToBlue combos with tuned probabilities, (3) the 3-state GoToBlue+stop+explore pattern with tighter parameters.

## Reasoning

The champion remains pure GoToColor blue (3714). The GoAwayGreen→GoToBlue 2-state machines (3793, 3802, 3808) are consistently the second-best family. The key pattern: GoAwayColor green (clr=2) on black floor transitions to GoToColor blue, with the blue-detection camera trigger. Stopping on black floor hurts. I should focus exploitation on fine-tuning the GoAwayGreen+GoToBlue probability parameters, and explore whether adding a third state helps or hurts.

## Reasoning

The key structure is: s0=GoAwayGreen transitions to s1=GoToBlue on black floor or blue seen; s1=GoToBlue transitions to s2=Explore on black floor, back to s0 on gray; s2=Explore transitions to s1 on blue seen, loops with small prob; s3=Stop transitions to s1 with tiny prob. Notably p2x1=0.03 and p2x1=0.015 both performed worse, confirming 0.02 is the sweet spot. p3x0=0.04 was worst, 0.06 was okay but not better than 0.05. I'll exploit the champion topology with very fine-grained parameter variations around the winning values, and try a few structural tweaks like adding a black-floor transition from s0 directly to s1, or varying the explore state behavior.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 16 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.46 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
